It's just strange, so that's why I figured I needed to come see someone.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these things—like holding the spoon up or folding the napkins over and over—do you feel like you have to do them, or can you stop yourself if you try?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm not rightly sure. I haven't really tried to stop. It's more like I don't even realize I'm stuck like that until someone says something to me. So I don't know if I feel like I have to do it, I just... I just don't think to stop myself while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes where you get stuck doing the same thing? Has this been going on for weeks, months, or years?\n\nPatient: Well, I reckon it's been about six months now. That's when folks started noticing this odd behavior, getting stuck like I said. And... well, they also tell me my talking has been a bit hard to follow lately, too. So, yeah, I'd say it's been going on for about the past six months.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you had any fevers, confusion, difficulty thinking clearly, or any major changes in your health or medications?\n\nPatient: No, Doctor, I haven't had any fevers or... or anything like that. My health has been pretty much the same as it always is. I haven't started any new medicines, either. And I don't feel confused, really. I just... I just get stuck doing things, like I told you. But no, no big changes in my health.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you feel like there's an urge or an impulse building up inside you that makes you want to do it, or does it just happen without any feeling like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of urge or... or anything building up like that. It's not like an itch I have to scratch. It just sort of happens, and I'm doing it without really feeling anything at all before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors, shaking, stiffness, or difficulty with your balance or walking over these past six months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My hands don't shake, and I haven't been stiff or had any trouble walking. My balance is just fine. It's just the... getting stuck, like I said. But no shaking or anything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's just strange, so that's why I figured I needed to come see someone.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these things—like holding the spoon up or folding the napkins over and over—do you feel like you have to do them, or can you stop yourself if you try?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm not rightly sure. I haven't really tried to stop. It's more like I don't even realize I'm stuck like that until someone says something to me. So I don't know if I feel like I have to do it, I just... I just don't think to stop myself while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes where you get stuck doing the same thing? Has this been going on for weeks, months, or years?\n\nPatient: Well, I reckon it's been about six months now. That's when folks started noticing this odd behavior, getting stuck like I said. And... well, they also tell me my talking has been a bit hard to follow lately, too. So, yeah, I'd say it's been going on for about the past six months.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you had any fevers, confusion, difficulty thinking clearly, or any major changes in your health or medications?\n\nPatient: No, Doctor, I haven't had any fevers or... or anything like that. My health has been pretty much the same as it always is. I haven't started any new medicines, either. And I don't feel confused, really. I just... I just get stuck doing things, like I told you. But no, no big changes in my health.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you feel like there's an urge or an impulse building up inside you that makes you want to do it, or does it just happen without any feeling like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of urge or... or anything building up like that. It's not like an itch I have to scratch. It just sort of happens, and I'm doing it without really feeling anything at all before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors, shaking, stiffness, or difficulty with your balance or walking over these past six months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My hands don't shake, and I haven't been stiff or had any trouble walking. My balance is just fine. It's just the... getting stuck, like I said. But no shaking or anything else.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck doing these things like folding the napkins or holding the spoon, are you making the same exact movement over and over again, or are you just frozen and not moving at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's actually both of those things. Like when I'm at church by the potluck table, I'll just stand there holding the serving spoon up in the air... and I'm not moving at all. Just completely frozen. But then with the napkins, I'll be folding the exact same stack over and over again, making the same movement. And people will just be standing there waiting on me while I do it. So I guess it just depends on what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these kinds of repetitive movements or getting stuck like this when you were younger, as a child or teenager, or did this all start just six months ago?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like this as a child or a teenager. I was always just fine back then. It all really just started about six months ago. The getting stuck and the odd behavior... and I guess people have been saying my talking is hard to follow lately, too. But yeah, all of that has just been noticeable for the past six months, not before that.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you first noticed the getting stuck and the odd behavior, did you have any serious infections, head injuries, or any major illness that required hospitalization or made you very sick?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't been sick or in the hospital or anything. I didn't hit my head. I've been pretty healthy, honestly, right up until all this started happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

So, yeah, I'd say it's been going on for about the past six months.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you had any fevers, confusion, difficulty thinking clearly, or any major changes in your health or medications?\n\nPatient: No, Doctor, I haven't had any fevers or... or anything like that. My health has been pretty much the same as it always is. I haven't started any new medicines, either. And I don't feel confused, really. I just... I just get stuck doing things, like I told you. But no, no big changes in my health.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you feel like there's an urge or an impulse building up inside you that makes you want to do it, or does it just happen without any feeling like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of urge or... or anything building up like that. It's not like an itch I have to scratch. It just sort of happens, and I'm doing it without really feeling anything at all before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors, shaking, stiffness, or difficulty with your balance or walking over these past six months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My hands don't shake, and I haven't been stiff or had any trouble walking. My balance is just fine. It's just the... getting stuck, like I said. But no shaking or anything else.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck doing these things like folding the napkins or holding the spoon, are you making the same exact movement over and over again, or are you just frozen and not moving at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's actually both of those things. Like when I'm at church by the potluck table, I'll just stand there holding the serving spoon up in the air... and I'm not moving at all. Just completely frozen. But then with the napkins, I'll be folding the exact same stack over and over again, making the same movement. And people will just be standing there waiting on me while I do it. So I guess it just depends on what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these kinds of repetitive movements or getting stuck like this when you were younger, as a child or teenager, or did this all start just six months ago?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like this as a child or a teenager. I was always just fine back then. It all really just started about six months ago. The getting stuck and the odd behavior... and I guess people have been saying my talking is hard to follow lately, too. But yeah, all of that has just been noticeable for the past six months, not before that.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you first noticed the getting stuck and the odd behavior, did you have any serious infections, head injuries, or any major illness that required hospitalization or made you very sick?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't been sick or in the hospital or anything. I didn't hit my head. I've been pretty healthy, honestly, right up until all this started happening.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you started getting stuck doing these things, did you have any severe headaches, body aches, or feel unusually tired or feverish, even for just a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like that. No bad headaches or body aches. I wasn't running a fever or feeling overly tired or anything. Like I said, I was feeling perfectly fine until all this weird stuff started happening about six months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, like feeling more depressed, anxious, or stressed over the past six months, or has anything major happened in your life around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, something major did happen. I suppose... well, this whole change has really been going on since my closest choir friend died. That was about 11 months ago now.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over or standing frozen with the spoon, do you lose track of time? Like, does it feel like only a few seconds have passed when actually it's been much longer, or do you have no memory of what happened during those times?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really lose track of time. I know I'm standing there. It doesn't feel like just a few seconds, I know it's taking a while. I remember it all, honestly. I just... I just get stuck doing it. Like, I'll be standing by the potluck table, just holding the serving spoon up in the air, and I just don't move. Or, well, I'll just stand there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again while folks are right there waiting on me. It's just strange, so that's why I figured I needed to come see someone.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these things—like holding the spoon up or folding the napkins over and over—do you feel like you have to do them, or can you stop yourself if you try?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I'm not rightly sure. I haven't really tried to stop. It's more like I don't even realize I'm stuck like that until someone says something to me. So I don't know if I feel like I have to do it, I just... I just don't think to stop myself while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes where you get stuck doing the same thing? Has this been going on for weeks, months, or years?\n\nPatient: Well, I reckon it's been about six months now. That's when folks started noticing this odd behavior, getting stuck like I said. And... well, they also tell me my talking has been a bit hard to follow lately, too. So, yeah, I'd say it's been going on for about the past six months.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you had any fevers, confusion, difficulty thinking clearly, or any major changes in your health or medications?\n\nPatient: No, Doctor, I haven't had any fevers or... or anything like that. My health has been pretty much the same as it always is. I haven't started any new medicines, either. And I don't feel confused, really. I just... I just get stuck doing things, like I told you. But no, no big changes in my health.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins or holding the spoon, do you feel like there's an urge or an impulse building up inside you that makes you want to do it, or does it just happen without any feeling like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel any kind of urge or... or anything building up like that. It's not like an itch I have to scratch. It just sort of happens, and I'm doing it without really feeling anything at all before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors, shaking, stiffness, or difficulty with your balance or walking over these past six months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My hands don't shake, and I haven't been stiff or had any trouble walking. My balance is just fine. It's just the... getting stuck, like I said. But no shaking or anything else.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck doing these things like folding the napkins or holding the spoon, are you making the same exact movement over and over again, or are you just frozen and not moving at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's actually both of those things. Like when I'm at church by the potluck table, I'll just stand there holding the serving spoon up in the air... and I'm not moving at all. Just completely frozen. But then with the napkins, I'll be folding the exact same stack over and over again, making the same movement. And people will just be standing there waiting on me while I do it. So I guess it just depends on what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these kinds of repetitive movements or getting stuck like this when you were younger, as a child or teenager, or did this all start just six months ago?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like this as a child or a teenager. I was always just fine back then. It all really just started about six months ago. The getting stuck and the odd behavior... and I guess people have been saying my talking is hard to follow lately, too. But yeah, all of that has just been noticeable for the past six months, not before that.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you first noticed the getting stuck and the odd behavior, did you have any serious infections, head injuries, or any major illness that required hospitalization or made you very sick?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't been sick or in the hospital or anything. I didn't hit my head. I've been pretty healthy, honestly, right up until all this started happening.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you started getting stuck doing these things, did you have any severe headaches, body aches, or feel unusually tired or feverish, even for just a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like that. No bad headaches or body aches. I wasn't running a fever or feeling overly tired or anything. Like I said, I was feeling perfectly fine until all this weird stuff started happening about six months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, like feeling more depressed, anxious, or stressed over the past six months, or has anything major happened in your life around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, something major did happen. I suppose... well, this whole change has really been going on since my closest choir friend died. That was about 11 months ago now.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over or standing frozen with the spoon, do you lose track of time? Like, does it feel like only a few seconds have passed when actually it's been much longer, or do you have no memory of what happened during those times?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really lose track of time. I know I'm standing there. It doesn't feel like just a few seconds, I know it's taking a while. I remember it all, honestly. I just... I just get stuck doing it. But I don't forget what's happening around me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck folding the napkins or holding the spoon, does this tend to happen more when you're feeling upset, stressed, or thinking about something that bothers you, or does it happen pretty much randomly no matter how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don't think it has to do with feeling upset. It just... it just happens. I could be having a perfectly normal day, just getting the table ready, and then I'm stuck on the napkins. I haven't really noticed it happening more when I'm stressed or thinking about things. It just seems to come on whenever.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I don't think I can make it stop. That's really why I came to see you, because at church I keep doing these odd things and I just can't seem to control it. Like I mentioned, I'll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just... not moving. Or I'll be folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over while people are right there waiting on me to finish. 

In my head, I know I need to put the spoon down or hand them the napkins, and I try real hard to tell myself to do it. But my body just won't listen. It just stays frozen or keeps folding, no matter how hard I concentrate on trying to stop.

Like when I'm at church by the potluck table, I'll just stand there holding the serving spoon up in the air... and I'm not moving at all. Just completely frozen. But then with the napkins, I'll be folding the exact same stack over and over again, making the same movement. And people will just be standing there waiting on me while I do it. So I guess it just depends on what I'm doing at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have these kinds of repetitive movements or getting stuck like this when you were younger, as a child or teenager, or did this all start just six months ago?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like this as a child or a teenager. I was always just fine back then. It all really just started about six months ago. The getting stuck and the odd behavior... and I guess people have been saying my talking is hard to follow lately, too. But yeah, all of that has just been noticeable for the past six months, not before that.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you first noticed the getting stuck and the odd behavior, did you have any serious infections, head injuries, or any major illness that required hospitalization or made you very sick?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't been sick or in the hospital or anything. I didn't hit my head. I've been pretty healthy, honestly, right up until all this started happening.\n\nDoctor: In the six months before you started getting stuck doing these things, did you have any severe headaches, body aches, or feel unusually tired or feverish, even for just a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't have anything like that. No bad headaches or body aches. I wasn't running a fever or feeling overly tired or anything. Like I said, I was feeling perfectly fine until all this weird stuff started happening about six months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood, like feeling more depressed, anxious, or stressed over the past six months, or has anything major happened in your life around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, something major did happen. I suppose... well, this whole change has really been going on since my closest choir friend died. That was about 11 months ago now.\n\nDoctor: When you're folding those napkins over and over or standing frozen with the spoon, do you lose track of time? Like, does it feel like only a few seconds have passed when actually it's been much longer, or do you have no memory of what happened during those times?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really lose track of time. I know I'm standing there. It doesn't feel like just a few seconds, I know it's taking a while. I remember it all, honestly. I just... I just get stuck doing it. But I don't forget what's happening around me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck folding the napkins or holding the spoon, does this tend to happen more when you're feeling upset, stressed, or thinking about something that bothers you, or does it happen pretty much randomly no matter how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: I don't think it has to do with feeling upset. It just... it just happens. I could be having a perfectly normal day, just getting the table ready, and then I'm stuck on the napkins. I haven't really noticed it happening more when I'm stressed or thinking about things. It just seems to come on whenever.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any viral infections in the past year or so—like the flu, meningitis, or anything where you had a high fever and stiff neck? Or has anyone ever mentioned autoimmune problems or anything affecting your brain or nervous system?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. I haven't had the flu or any bad fevers or... stiff necks, no. And no doctor has ever said anything to me about autoimmune problems or anything wrong with my brain or nervous system. As far as I know, I haven't been sick with any of that.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your choir friend passed away about 11 months ago, and the getting stuck started about 6 months ago. Between when your friend died and when you first noticed yourself getting stuck, did anything else significant happen in your life, or did the stuck episodes start pretty soon after you were grieving?\n\nPatient: No, not really... nothing else happened. It was just, well, regular days. Nothing big changed in my life between when she passed away and when I started getting stuck like that. I was still grieving her, I suppose, just missing her... but no, there weren't any other big events or anything.\n\nDoctor: If someone gently touched your arm or called your name while you were frozen holding the spoon, do you think you could snap out of it right away, or would you stay stuck until it just naturally stopped?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure. I live alone, so... well, there's nobody around to touch my arm or call my name when it happens. It's just me in the house. So I don't know if that would snap me out of it or not. I've just always had to wait until it passes on its own.\n\nDoctor: When you're stuck folding the napkins or holding the spoon, if you really concentrate hard and try your very best to stop what you're doing, do you think you could make yourself stop, or does your body just keep going no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: Well... I don't think I can make it stop. That's really why I came to see you, because at church I keep doing these odd things and I just can't seem to control it. Like I mentioned, I'll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just... not moving. Or I'll be folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over while people are right there waiting on me to finish. \n\nIn my head, I know I need to put the spoon down or hand them the napkins, and I try real hard to tell myself to do it. But my body just won't listen. It just stays frozen or keeps folding, no matter how hard I concentrate on trying to stop.\n\nDoctor: If someone were to gently shake your shoulder or tap your arm while you're frozen holding that spoon, do you think that would break through and get your attention, or would you stay completely stuck until it stops on its own?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a minute ago... I'm just really not sure. Even at the church with the serving spoon, people are right there waiting in line, but nobody has actually come up and shaken my shoulder or tapped me yet. I think they just kind of watch me and don't know what to do. \n\nSo I really couldn't tell you if that would snap me out of it or if I'd just stay frozen. It just hasn't happened where somebody tried to touch me while I was stuck.\n\nDoctor: If I were sitting right here with you and you got stuck holding that spoon, and I stood up and clapped my hands loudly right in front of your face, do you think that loud noise would snap you out of it, or would you stay completely frozen no matter what?\n\nPatient: Well, I honestly just don't know. Like I keep saying, nobody has ever tried anything like that when I get stuck. \n\nI suppose a loud clap right in my face might startle me, but... I really couldn't tell you for sure if it would actually unfreeze my body or if I'd just stay stuck there. Since nobody has ever done it, I just don't have a good answer for you.\n\nDoctor: I understand nobody has actually tried it yet. But let me ask you this differently: when you're frozen holding that spoon, can you hear people talking around you? Can you understand what they're saying even though you can't move?\n\nPatient: Well... I hear them, yes. I can hear the noise of folks talking around the table. But as for understanding what they're saying... I'm just not sure. I haven't really noticed. \n\nI guess my mind is just so wrapped up in trying to make my arm put the spoon down, I'm not really paying attention to their words. So I hear the sound of them talking, but I couldn't tell you what they were actually saying."
